=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a positive integer N and two strings S and T, each of length N and consisting of lowercase English letters.
Determine whether it is possible to make S identical to T by repeating the operation below any number of times (possibly zero). If it is possible, also find the minimum number of operations required.

- Choose two lowercase English letters x, y and replace every occurrence of x in S with y.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
S
T

Output

If it is possible to make S identical to T, print the minimum number of operations required. Otherwise, print -1.

Constraints


- 1\leq N \leq 2\times 10^5
- N is an integer.
- Each of S and T is a string of length N, consisting of lowercase English letters.

Sample Input 1

6
afbfda
bkckbb

Sample Output 1

4

By performing the operation four times in the following way, you can make S identical to T:

- Choose x= b and y= c. S becomes afcfda.
- Choose x= a and y= b. S becomes bfcfdb.
- Choose x= f and y= k. S becomes bkckdb.
- Choose x= d and y= b. S becomes bkckbb, which is identical to T.

It cannot be done with fewer than four operations, so the minimum number of operations required is 4.

Sample Input 2

4
abac
abac

Sample Output 2

0

S and T are already identical, so no operations are required.

Sample Input 3

4
abac
abrc

Sample Output 3

-1

No matter how you repeat the operation, it is impossible to make S identical to T.

Sample Input 4

4
abac
bcba

Sample Output 4

4

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's prove: In a functional graph on finite set, every component has exactly one directed cycle. If every node is in a cycle, then each component is just a cycle (no trees). Now, let's think of a formal algorithm to compute answer without constructing sequence. We can just compute:
- conflict check
- if len(P)==26 and len(set(T))==26 and any(map[c]!=c for c in P): print(-1)
- ans = number of c in P with map[c] != c
- add number of cycles in directed graph on 26 nodes with edges c -> map[c] for c in P, considering only cycles of length >=2? Need count cycles in the functional graph formed by all 26 letters where edges from c in P to map[c]. For c not in P, no outgoing edge (or maybe self? no). A cycle of length >=2 consists of letters in P. How to count cycles efficiently? Alphabet size 26, can do DFS/visited.

But careful: If we add edges for all c in P, including self-loops, then a self-loop is a cycle of length 1. We should not count it. For length >=2, count cycles. Since outdegree of each node in P is 1, nodes not in P outdegree 0. We can traverse.

Simplest: For each c from 0..25:
- if map[c] == -1: continue (no edge)
- if map[c] == c: self-loop, not count.
- else follow edges with visited states to detect cycles.

Because alphabet small, we can use state 0=unvisited,1=visiting,2=done. For each node with edge, DFS. Count when encounter visiting node as a cycle. But need count each cycle once. Standard:
visited = [0]*26
cycles=0
for i in range(26):
    if map[i] != -1 and visited[i]==0:
        cur=i
        path=[]
        while cur != -1 and visited[cur]==0:
            visited[cur]=1
            path.append(cur)
            cur = map[cur] if map[cur]!=-1 else -1
        if cur != -1 and visited[cur]==1:
            # found cycle starting at cur in path
            # count if cycle length >=2? Need identify cycle nodes.
            # Since cur is in path, cycle consists of path from index of cur to end.
            # If len(cycle)==1 and map[cur]==cur, don't count.
            # But if len(cycle)==1 and map[cur]!=cur? impossible because cur->cur.
            idx = path.index(cur)
            cycle = path[idx:]
            if len(cycle) >= 2:
                cycles += 1
        for node in path:
            visited[node]=2
But if we set visited=2 after processing, and later encounter a node that points into a previously processed cycle, no count. Good.

However, there is a subtlety: If a node not in P has map -1, but a P node maps to it, following cur = map[cur] gives -1, loop stops. Good.

But wait, if map[c] = d where d not in P, then d has map[d] = -1. So edge c->d is a sink. No cycle.

Now, if map[c] = d and d in P, follow. Good.

Could there be a cycle of length 2 where one node has map -1? No.

Now, if P=26 and mapping non-injective, there may be a self-loop at a node with indegree >1. We don't count self-loop. Good.

Now, let's verify with a brute-force mental search for small alphabet A=3, P=3, mapping non-injective with cycle. Example A=3, P={a,b,c}, mapping a->b,b->a,c->a. Formula changed3 + cycle1 =4. Is it possible in 3? Let's try all possible sequences of length 3 maybe. Initial labels a,b,c. Final a=b,b=a,c=a.
Operation 1 must be something. If we merge c->b (as we did), labels a,b (b+c), c empty. Then need swap a and b group. With empty c, swap takes 3 ops: a->c, b->a, c->b. Total 4. Final orig a=b, orig b=a, orig c=a. Operations: b->c, a->b, c->a =3! Wait is this valid? Let's simulate carefully:
Initial S groups: a, b, c.
Operation 1: choose x=b, y=c. Replace every b with c. Current labels: a, c, c (orig b and c merged at c). b empty.
Operation 2: choose x=a, y=b. Replace a with b. Current labels: b (orig a), c (orig b+c). a empty.
Operation 3: choose x=c, y=a. Replace c with a. Current labels: b (orig a), a (orig b+c).
Final: orig a -> b, orig b -> a, orig c -> a. T expected: a->b, b->a, c->a. Yes! Only 3 operations. But formula gave 4! Wait, this is a counterexample! Let's check mapping: S contains a,b,c. T has positions: a->b, b->a, c->a. So T string maybe "baa" if S="abc". Is it possible in 3? We just found sequence b->c, a->b, c->a. Let's verify operation 1 b->c: S "abc" -> "acc"? Actually replace b with c: "a c c". Operation 2 a->b: "b c c". Operation 3 c->a: "b a a". T = "b a a". Yes! 3 operations. Our formula #changed=3 (a,b,c) + cycles1 =4 is wrong! Important.

Let's analyze. Mapping a->b, b->a, c->a. Graph: a<->b cycle, c->a. We found 3 operations, not 4. Why did earlier think 4? Because we tried to preserve cycle a,b and used temp, but we can merge c with b first, then use b? Wait sequence:
b->c merges original b with c (both final a). This destroys the cycle? Original b is no longer at b; b becomes empty. Then a->b moves original a to b. Then c->a moves merged b+c to a. Final: a at b, b+c at a. This effectively swapped a with the merged group using b as temp? Let's count operations:
1 b->c: original b changes from b to c (merged with c). This is b's changed operation and also c? c didn't change (still c). It creates empty b.

Let's re-evaluate. This is crucial.

We need find true minimum.

Let's analyze operation sequence in terms of graph. Mapping a->b, b->a, c->a. Graph has cycle a,b and edge c->a (tail into cycle). We did:
b -> c (move cycle node b to tail node c, merging with tail)
a -> b (move a to b)
c -> a (move merged tail+b to a)
This is like: choose a cycle node b, move it to a node c that is not in the cycle and has same final target as b? Wait b final a, c final a. So b and c can merge. Then b's original label becomes empty, allowing a to move to b. Then merged group moves to a. This uses the fact that there is a tail node with same image as a cycle node. The cycle node b didn't need a separate temp; it moved to its final target indirectly by merging with c, and the empty b served as temp for a. So the extra operation for the cycle is avoided because one cycle node's final target equals the final target of a tail node, allowing it to vacate its label by merging into the tail node. Is that always possible? Let's generalize.

We need solve minimum operations exactly.

Let's model the process more abstractly.

We have original letters P. Operation x->y:
- If y is currently empty, it's a rename of label x to y; x becomes empty.
- If y is occupied, it merges the group at x into group at y; x becomes empty.

We need final mapping g. We can think of each original letter c as a token that starts at label c and must end at label g(c). Tokens can merge only if they have same final label (since merged tokens cannot split). Operation x->y moves all tokens currently at label x to label y. If y has tokens, merge. This is exactly sorting tokens on labels with operations "move all tokens from one label to another". We have 26 labels, some initially empty. Need transform initial token configuration (one token per letter in P, at its own label) to final configuration where for each label d, tokens with g(c)=d are at d. Tokens with same final label can be merged at some point; they don't need remain separate. Actually if multiple original letters map to same d, they can merge into one group and end at d. So the final "groups" correspond to equivalence classes of g: for each target d, all c with g(c)=d form one group (if nonempty). We don't need distinguish them. Thus the problem is: we have tokens initially one per label in P. We need merge tokens that have same final target (if any) and move each group to its target label. Operation moves all tokens at a source label to a target label, merging if target occupied.

This is reminiscent of sorting by "transpositions" where moving a set from x to y. We want min operations.

We can ignore letters not in P as empty labels. There are E = 26 - |P| empty labels initially. If E>0, we have at least one empty.

Let's define final target function f(c)=g(c) for c in P. For c not in P, no token, but labels can be used.

If f is injective (no merges needed), then each token must end at a distinct label. This is a permutation from P to Q (|Q|=|P|). If E>0, min operations? For a permutation, no merges allowed. Operation must move tokens to empty labels only. This is like sorting a permutation using empty slots. Minimum number of moves? For a cycle of length k, need k+1 moves if there is an empty slot. For identity, 0. So answer = #nonfixed + #cycles. This matched a->b,b->a with empty c: 3.

If f is non-injective, merges are allowed. Merging can reduce number of tokens/groups. We can merge tokens with same final target. This can create empty labels and reduce operations. In example a->b,b->a,c->a, f non-injective (b,c -> a). We merged b and c (same target a), creating empty b, then moved a to b, then merged group to a. Total 3 = #changed. So cycles may not add if there is a mergeable pair involving a cycle node? Let's explore.

We need find general formula. This is a known problem: Given a directed graph of required mapping, min operations = number of vertices v with f(v) != v plus number of cycles that are "bad" maybe. Let's derive from first principles.

We have tokens with final targets. Operation x->y can be viewed as:
- If x is empty, no effect (ignore).
- If y is empty, it's a move of group from x to y. This decreases? Number of occupied labels stays same, empty label moves from y to x.
- If y is occupied, it merges group at x into y. Number of occupied labels decreases by 1, empty label x appears.

We need reach final occupied labels: for each target d in Q, one group at d (containing all tokens with target d). Actually if target d equals some original label that is not occupied final? It is occupied. We don't care about labels not in Q; they should be empty.

Initial occupied labels P. Final occupied labels Q. If |Q| < |P|, at least |P|-|Q| merges must occur. Each merge operation reduces occupied count by 1. Also, moves to empty labels keep occupied count same.

Lower bound: Each operation can at most reduce "distance" or change one token group? Let's think in terms of occupied labels count.

Let m = |P|, q = |Q|. Since each merge reduces occupied count by 1, and final occupied count q, number of merge operations must be exactly m - q? Wait operation x->y with y occupied reduces occupied count by 1. Operation to empty keeps count same. Initially occupied count m, final q. Since occupied count never increases, and each merge reduces by 1, the total number of merge operations in any valid sequence is exactly m - q. Is it possible to have more merges then later increase? No increase, so to end at q, exactly m-q merges. Thus any valid sequence has exactly M = |P| - |Q| merge operations. In example a->b,b->a,c->a: m=3, q=2 (targets b,a), M=1. Sequence had one merge: b->c (b merged with c) or c->a? Wait b->c merged b into c (occupied), yes one merge. Total ops 3. Moves to empty: a->b (b empty after merge), c->a (a empty after a->b? Let's see after a->b, a empty, so c->a moves to empty? Actually c occupied, a empty, so move to empty. Total moves to empty 2. Merge 1. Total 3.

For injective permutation m=q, M=0, no merges. Need moves to empty only. For a cycle of length k, need k+1 moves (one extra). For identity, 0.

So total operations = (m - q) + (number of moves to empty). We need minimize moves to empty subject to realizing f. Since merges count fixed.

This perspective helps. Each operation is either a merge (target occupied) or a move (target empty). Merges are necessary to reduce groups. We want minimize moves to empty. A move to empty changes the label of a group from x to an empty label e; x becomes empty. This is like moving a token/group into a "hole" (empty label). We have holes (empty labels). Initially holes = 26 - m. After each merge, holes increases by 1. After each move, hole moves from target to source. Final holes = 26 - q.

We need schedule merges and moves. The number of moves to empty is total ops - merges. We need minimize moves. Since merges fixed, minimizing ops = minimizing moves.

This is analogous to sorting with holes. For a permutation (m=q, no merges), holes = 26-m. If there is at least one hole, each nontrivial cycle requires one extra move? Actually for a cycle of length k, with one hole, moves = k+1. For multiple cycles, can reuse same hole, moves = (#nonfixed) + (#cycles). So moves = changed + cycles. Total ops same.

When merges exist, holes can be created by merges. A merge operation itself is not a move; it reduces occupied labels and creates a hole. This hole can be used to resolve cycles, potentially avoiding extra moves. In example a->b,b->a,c->a: m=3,q=2,M=1. Initial holes = 23 (if A=26) but in A=3 example holes=0? Wait A=3, P={a,b,c}, m=3, q=2, initial holes=0. M=1 creates one hole. Moves to empty: 2. Total ops=3. #changed=3. So moves = changed? Actually changed=3, total ops=3, M=1, moves=2. #changed includes letters whose final target differs; all three changed. Moves to empty count 2, not 3. The merge operation changed b (and c? b changed from b to c, c not changed yet). So #changed not equal moves.

Now, need confirm "pure cycle" definition: A directed cycle of length >=2 such that no node in the cycle has incoming edges from outside the cycle. In a functional graph, if a cycle node has any incoming from outside, then that outside node is in the same component and not on the cycle, so component size > cycle length. If no outside incoming, then the component is exactly the cycle (because every node in component reaches the cycle; if no outside, no other nodes). So pure cycle = component that is a simple directed cycle of length >=2. We can count such components.

Now, let's formulate algorithm:

1. Read N, S, T.
2. Initialize map[26] = [-1]*26.
3. For each i:
   c = ord(S[i])-97; d = ord(T[i])-97.
   if map[c] == -1: map[c] = d
   elif map[c] != d: print(-1); return
4. Build set P = letters appearing in S. (Could use boolean presentS)
5. ans = 0
   For c in 0..25:
      if presentS[c] and map[c] != c: ans += 1
6. Count bad cycles:
   We need count components that are simple directed cycles of length >=2.
   We can do DFS on graph edges c -> map[c] for c in presentS. Since alphabet 26, simple.

But careful: A component that is a pure cycle length >=2 has no incoming from outside. In functional graph, if we start DFS from any node in the cycle, we traverse the cycle and return. If there are tails, starting from a tail will eventually reach cycle; starting from cycle node, we also traverse cycle and return. Standard cycle detection will find the cycle, but we need know if the cycle has any incoming from outside (i.e., component size > cycle length). We can compute component size and cycle length. If component size == cycle length and cycle length >=2, then it's a pure cycle. If component size > cycle length, not bad. If cycle length ==1, not bad.

Because alphabet small, we can for each unvisited node with presentS, traverse to collect component nodes and detect cycle. Then compute cycle length and component size. Or simpler: For each node, determine if it is on a cycle of length >=2 and has no incoming from outside. We can compute indegree in the graph restricted to P? Let's define indegree[c] = number of d in P such that map[d]==c. For a pure cycle of length >=2, every node in the cycle has indegree exactly 1 (from predecessor) and no outside incoming; component size = cycle length. If a cycle node has indegree >1, there is a tail, so not pure. If a cycle node has indegree 1 but there are tails attached to non-cycle nodes? Example cycle a->b,b->a, tail c->b. Then b indegree2, so not pure. If tail attaches to a non-cycle node that eventually reaches cycle, the cycle nodes still indegree1? Example a->b,b->a, c->d, d->a. Cycle nodes a,b indegree? a has from b and d? d->a, so a indegree2. So not pure. If tail chain attaches to a non-cycle node that is not directly to cycle? It eventually reaches cycle, so the cycle node at the attachment has indegree from the chain's last node, so >1. Thus any tail in component implies some cycle node indegree >1. Conversely, if all cycle nodes indegree exactly 1, no tails, component pure. So we can count cycles where every node on the cycle has indegree == 1? Wait in a pure cycle, each cycle node indegree exactly 1. In a component with a self-loop and no tails, self-loop node indegree1 but length1. In a component with self-loop and tail, self-loop node indegree2. So condition for bad cycle: cycle length >=2 and all nodes in the cycle have indegree == 1. But is it possible for a cycle node to have indegree 1 but there is a tail attached to a non-cycle node that eventually reaches the cycle? If tail chain attaches to a non-cycle node, the last node before cycle points to a cycle node, giving that cycle node indegree from that last node in addition to predecessor. So indegree >1. Thus no.

So we can detect cycles and check if any node in cycle has indegree >1. If none, bad.

But wait, what about a cycle of length >=2 where a cycle node has indegree 1, but there is a tail attached to a different cycle node? That would make that cycle node indegree >1. So no.

Thus algorithm:
- Compute indegree for each letter: for c in presentS, indegree[map[c]] += 1. Note map[c] may be -1? No, if c in presentS, map[c] set.
- Find cycles in graph. For each cycle of length >=2, check if all nodes in cycle have indegree == 1. If yes, ans += 1.

But is it possible for a cycle node to have indegree 1 but the component has extra nodes not attached to the cycle? In a functional graph, every node in the component reaches the cycle; if there is an extra node, it eventually attaches to some cycle node, increasing indegree. So no.

Let's test with a->b,b->a,c->a: cycle a,b. indegree a: from b and c =2; b: from a=1. Not all indegree1, so not bad. ans=changed3 +0=3. Good.

Now, let's examine the operation count formula in terms of graph and indegree. We can compute bad cycles as cycles where every node on the cycle has indegree == 1. But what about a cycle length >=2 where a cycle node has indegree 1 but there is a self-loop elsewhere? irrelevant. We count cycles individually.

Let's implement cycle detection robustly.

Because alphabet size 26, we can do:
visited = [0]*26
bad = 0
for i in range(26):
    if presentS[i] and visited[i] == 0:
        # traverse
        cur = i
        path = []
        while cur != -1 and visited[cur] == 0:
            visited[cur] = 1
            path.append(cur)
            cur = map[cur] if map[cur] != -1 else -1
        if cur != -1 and visited[cur] == 1:
            # found cycle
            idx = path.index(cur)
            cycle = path[idx:]
            if len(cycle) >= 2:
                # check if all nodes in cycle have indegree == 1
                if all(indegree[v] == 1 for v in cycle):
                    bad += 1
        for v in path:
            visited[v] = 2
But note: If we set visited[cur]=1 and later encounter cur that is already visited=1 from current DFS, cycle found. If cur is visited=2, no cycle. Good.

However, if we traverse from a node in a pure cycle, path will be the cycle and cur will be the first node, cycle found. If we traverse from a tail, path includes tail then cycle nodes; when cur is a cycle node already visited=1, path.index(cur) gives cycle start, cycle = path[idx:] includes cycle nodes only? Wait if starting from tail, path = [tail, ..., cycle_node1, cycle_node2, ..., cycle_node1]. The cycle in path is from first occurrence of cycle_node1 to end. It includes exactly cycle nodes. Good. If starting from a node in the cycle, path = [cycle_node1, cycle_node2, ..., cycle_node1], cycle includes all. Good.

But if we start from a tail and the cycle was already visited from previous DFS, then when we reach cycle node, visited[cur] may be 2, so no cycle counted. That's fine because we counted it when starting from the cycle earlier? But what if we started from a tail before visiting the cycle? Then we count it. If we start from a cycle node, count. Either way once.

Need ensure we don't count a cycle twice if we start from different nodes in same component? The DFS loop visits each node once; the first time a cycle is encountered, we count. Later nodes in same component are visited=2, no count. Good.

But there is a subtlety: If we start from a tail, path includes tail and cycle. We set visited for all path to 2 after counting. Good.

Now, indegree computation:
indeg = [0]*26
for c in range(26):
    if presentS[c]:
        indeg[map[c]] += 1
map[c] should not be -1 for presentS[c]. Good.

Now, ans = changed + bad.

    print(-1); return
But if map identity, any false. If map non-identity, -1. Is it possible len(P)==26, len(set(T))==26, map non-identity but there is no bad cycle? No, but condition okay.

Now, let's think about if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail is not a direct child of the cycle; we merge predecessor into the direct child. The direct child u may have children. We process cycle and then tree. The total operations for the component = number of nodes in component (all changed). Let's prove by induction:
- For a tree attached to a cycle, process nodes in order from cycle outward: each node moves to its parent. This includes the direct child u? Actually u is merged with predecessor cycle node and then moves to cycle node a_j. That's u's operation (u->a_j) after cycle moves? Wait in our sequence, the direct child u's token is at label u, and we merged a_{j-1}->u. Later we move u group -> a_j. That operation changes u from u to a_j (and a_{j-1} from u to a_j). So u's changed operation is the final move u->a_j. The merge a_{j-1}->u is a_{j-1}'s changed operation? Actually a_{j-1} changed from a_{j-1} to u in merge, then later to a_j in u->a_j. So a_{j-1} required two operations, while u required one. Total for those two nodes =2 operations (merge + final move), equal to number of nodes (a_{j-1}, u). The cycle moves account for other cycle nodes. So each node in component is associated with one operation? Let's map:
- Merge a_{j-1}->u: operation for a_{j-1} (first change).
- Cycle moves a_i -> a_{i+1} for i = j-2 down to j: operations for those a_i.
- Final u->a_j: operation for u (and second for a_{j-1}).
- Tree children moves: operations for each child.
Total operations = n. Good.

Total = changed_nonpure + changed_badcycle +1. If we somehow use the hole creation operation as part of the bad cycle? The hole creation is a merge in another component; it doesn't move any token in the bad cycle. The bad cycle needs k+1 moves, so extra1. So yes.

Cycle a->b,b->c,c->a. Tail d->a, e->d. So nodes a,b,c,d,e. Changed5. Formula bad? cycle indegree a=2 (c,d), not bad, ans5.

For nontrivial cycle, merging predecessor into tail breaks the cycle and creates hole at predecessor's label, which is the target of the previous cycle node, allowing a chain of moves without extra. Merging tail into predecessor leaves cycle intact and requires extra. So we should merge predecessor into tail. So for self-loop, merge tail into cycle node. For nontrivial cycle, merge predecessor into tail. Both are safe and create a hole. The key is to create a hole that is the target of the next node to move, enabling a path.

Our construction for nontrivial cycle with tails uses merge predecessor into tail. For self-loop, there is no "previous" cycle node distinct; we can just process tail into self-loop.

Does our formula still hold? Yes, because for nontrivial cycle with tails, we can achieve changed ops. For self-loop with tails, changed ops. The direction choice doesn't affect count.

Pseudo:

import sys

def solve():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N = int(input())
    S = input().strip()
    T = input().strip()
    mp = [-1]*26
    present = [False]*26
    for ch in S:
        present[ord(ch)-97] = True
    # conflict
    for a,b in zip(S,T):
        c = ord(a)-97; d = ord(b)-97
        if mp[c] == -1:
            mp[c] = d
        elif mp[c] != d:
            print(-1)
            return
    # If any present letter has mp -1? Shouldn't.
    # distinct counts
    cntS = sum(present)
    cntT = len(set(T))
    # impossible if all 26 letters in S and T and not identity
    if cntS == 26 and cntT == 26:
        # mapping is permutation; if any mp[c] != c for present c, impossible
        # Since cntS=26, all c present.
        if any(mp[c] != c for c in range(26)):
            print(-1)
            return
    # changed
    ans = 0
    for c in range(26):
        if present[c] and mp[c] != c:
            ans += 1
    # indegree
    indeg = [0]*26
    for c in range(26):
        if present[c]:
            indeg[mp[c]] += 1
    # count bad cycles
    visited = [0]*26
    bad = 0
    for i in range(26):
        if present[i] and visited[i] == 0:
            cur = i
            path = []
            while cur != -1 and visited[cur] == 0:
                visited[cur] = 1
                path.append(cur)
                nxt = mp[cur] if mp[cur] != -1 else -1
                cur = nxt
            if cur != -1 and visited[cur] == 1:
                # find cycle
                try:
                    idx = path.index(cur)
                except ValueError:
                    idx = -1
                if idx != -1:
                    cycle = path[idx:]
                    if len(cycle) >= 2:
                        if all(indeg[v] == 1 for v in cycle):
                            bad += 1
            for v in path:
                visited[v] = 2
    ans += bad
    print(ans)

Need ensure in DFS, if cur becomes -1, loop stops. If cur is a node not present, mp[cur] = -1, but cur could be a target not in S. We set cur = -1 after mp[cur]? Now, let's think about if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the direct child u's final target a_j is also the target of a tail w. w can merge into a_j at end. Good.

Now, let's consider if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but there is no initial empty and P=26. Our construction starts with a merge, which creates a hole. No issue.

Now, let's think about if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u is not changed? u->a_j, u != a_j, so changed. Good.

Now, let's consider if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the cycle length is 2 and the direct child u maps to a_j. We merge predecessor into u. Example a->b,b->a,u->a. Predecessor of a is b. Merge b->u. Then process a->b, u->a. Total 3. Good.

Now, let's think about if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u is the same as the predecessor? no.

Now, let's consider if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has indegree from a child v. We process u->a_j before v->u. Good.

Now, let's think about if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has a child v that maps to u, and v has a child w. Process u, v, w. Good.

Now, let's consider if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u is not a leaf, but we merge a_{j-1}->u. This means u label contains a_{j-1}+u. Later u->a_j moves both. Then v->u moves v to u. But v final u, correct. Good.

Now, let's think about if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has a child v that maps to u, and v is also the predecessor of some cycle? no.

Now, let's consider if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u is the only node in the tree. We did.

Now, let's think about if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has final target a_j. What if a_j is also the predecessor's target? Yes.

Now, let's consider if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u is not the only child; we can choose any direct child. If there are multiple, choose one. Good.

Now, let's think about if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has a child v that maps to u, and v is the direct child of the cycle? No, v maps to u, not cycle.

Now, let's consider if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u is also the target of a node on the cycle? impossible.

Now, let's think about if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has final target a_j. We merge a_{j-1}->u. This operation changes a_{j-1} from a_{j-1} to u. But a_{j-1} final a_j. Later u->a_j changes it to a_j. Good.

Now, let's consider if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has final target a_j. What if a_{j-1}->u is not allowed because u is not a lowercase letter? It is.

Now, let's think about if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has final target a_j. If u is not present? It is present because it's a node in P.

Now, let's consider if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has final target a_j. The operation a_{j-1}->u merges a_{j-1} into u. But u's final target is a_j, and a_{j-1}'s final target is a_j. Safe.

Now, let's think about if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has final target a_j. If u has children, they final u, not a_j. They are not at u yet, so merging a_{j-1} into u doesn't merge with children. Later children move to u after u leaves. Safe.

Now, let's consider if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has final target a_j. If u has a child v, after u->a_j, v->u. But what if v also has a child w, after v->u, w->v. Good.

Now, let's think about if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has final target a_j. If there is a tail w directly to a_{j-1}, after cycle processing a_{j-1} contains a_{j-2}, so w->a_{j-1} merges. Safe. If w has children, process after w. Good.

Now, let's consider if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has final target a_j. If there is a tail w directly to u? That's a child of u, processed after u. Good.

Now, let's think about if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has final target a_j. If there is a tail w directly to a_j, after u->a_j, a_j contains predecessor+u. w->a_j merges. Safe.

Now, let's consider if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has final target a_j. If there is a tail w directly to a_{j-2}, after cycle processing a_{j-2} is empty? Let's trace final labels: After processing, a_{j-2} contains original a_{j-3}? Wait final for cycle: a_i contains original a_{i-1} (with indices mod k) except a_j? Let's check k=3,j=1 final: a1 contains a3+u, a2 contains a1, a3 contains a2. So a_{j-2}=a2 contains a1, not empty. A tail w -> a2 final a2 can merge into a2. Safe. If w has children, process after w. Good.

Now, let's think about if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has final target a_j. If there is a tail w directly to a_{j-1}, after cycle processing a_{j-1} contains a_{j-2}. w->a_{j-1} merges. Safe.

Now, let's consider if a component has a nontrivial cycle and a tail, but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has final target a_j. If there is a tail w directly to a_{j+1}, after cycle processing a_{j+1} contains a_j? Wait a_{j+1} final target? Let's not overcomplicate. All tree nodes can be processed after cycle by moving each node to its parent, in order from cycle outward. The parent labels are occupied by their final groups, so merges are safe. This is a standard tree processing. It works regardless of which cycle node they attach to.

Thus construction solid.

Now, let's think about lower bound for changed. We already argued each changed letter must be source of at least one operation. Is that always true if a changed letter merges into another before its label changes? To merge, it must be source of an operation. Example original c final d != c. The first operation affecting group c has source c (since before that group label c). So yes. Thus ops >= changed.

For bad cycles, need extra. Suppose a component is a pure cycle of length k. No merges within component; no outside node maps into it. Could there be an outside merge that creates a hole and also changes a cycle node? No, outside merge doesn't change cycle nodes. To realize the cycle, each cycle node's group must move. With one hole, k+1 moves. Could a cycle node be changed by an operation whose source is not its original label? The first change of each cycle node source is its original label, so k operations at least. But to resolve cyclic dependency, at least one more operation with source a hole (or a cycle node second time) is needed. This is standard. So bad cycle adds at least 1. If there are multiple bad cycles, each adds at least 1 because cycles are disjoint and no merge between them; the extra operation for one cycle cannot help another? Could the same extra operation help two cycles? Suppose one operation with source hole h moves a token from cycle1 to its target, and h becomes some label. Could that label be used as a hole for cycle2, but the operation itself is still one extra for cycle1. Cycle2 still needs its own extra move. The total extra at least number of bad cycles. Good.

Now, let's think about if a bad cycle of length k and another bad cycle of length m can share a single extra operation by moving a token from cycle1 into a hole that is a token from cycle2? They are disjoint; no token from cycle2 is a hole initially. To create a hole in cycle2, need an extra move. So no.

Now, let's consider if a bad cycle can be resolved without extra if there is a letter not in P that is also a final target of a node outside the cycle, and that outside node can be moved into the cycle? No, moving outside node into cycle would merge with cycle node, final targets differ unless outside node maps to that cycle node. If outside node maps to a cycle node, then the cycle is not pure (indegree >1). So not bad. Thus bad cycles require extra.

Now, let's think about if a component with a nontrivial cycle and a tail might still require extra if the tail is not "usable" because it has children that need to move before? We found construction with no extra. So no.

Now, let's consider if a component with a nontrivial cycle and a tail but the tail's direct child u is also the predecessor's target? No.

Now, let's think about if a component with a nontrivial cycle and a tail but the tail's direct child u has final target a_j, and predecessor a_{j-1} final a_j. We merge a_{j-1}->u. What if u is not a direct child of the cycle but deeper? We choose the direct child along the path. Good.

Now, let's consider if a component with a nontrivial cycle and a tail but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has final target a_j. If u has indegree from a child v, and v has final target u. After u->a_j, v->u. But v's operation is a move to empty u, not a merge. It changes v to u. Good. If v has child w, w->v after v moves. Good.

Now, let's think about if a component with a nontrivial cycle and a tail but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has final target a_j. If u has indegree from multiple children, each can move to u after u leaves. Good.

Now, let's consider if a component with a nontrivial cycle and a tail but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has final target a_j. If u is also the target of a cycle node? No.

Now, let's think about if a component with a nontrivial cycle and a tail but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has final target a_j. If u is the same as a_j? no.

Now, let's consider if a component with a nontrivial cycle and a tail but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has final target a_j. If there is a self-loop at u? Then u would be a cycle, not tail. no.

The merge is one of the required merges. Good.

Now, let's think about if a component with a nontrivial cycle and a tail but the tail's path enters the cycle at a_j, and the direct child u has final target a_j. Then x->w moves to empty w. Good. If w had multiple children, they move to w after. Good.

Now, let's move on. We need produce final code only. But before final, let's ensure no issue with input format: N line, S, T. Use sys.stdin.read().split() simpler.

Code:

import sys

def solve():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data: return
    N = int(data[0])
    S = data[1].strip()
    T = data[2].strip()
    mp = [-1]*26
    present = [False]*26
    for ch in S:
        present[ord(ch)-97] = True
    for a,b in zip(S,T):
        c = ord(a)-97; d = ord(b)-97
        if mp[c] == -1:
            mp[c] = d
        elif mp[c] != d:
            print(-1)
            return
    cntS = sum(present)
    cntT = len(set(T))
    if cntS == 26 and cntT == 26:
        if any(mp[c] != c for c in range(26)):
            print(-1)
            return
    ans = 0
    for c in range(26):
        if present[c] and mp[c] != c:
            ans += 1
    indeg = [0]*26
    for c in range(26):
        if present[c]:
            indeg[mp[c]] += 1
    visited = [0]*26
    bad = 0
    for i in range(26):
        if present[i] and visited[i] == 0:
            cur = i
            path = []
            while cur != -1 and visited[cur] == 0:
                visited[cur] = 1
                path.append(cur)
                cur = mp[cur] if mp[cur] != -1 else -1
            if cur != -1 and visited[cur] == 1:
                idx = path.index(cur)  # cur is in path
                cycle = path[idx:]
                if len(cycle) >= 2:
                    if all(indeg[v] == 1 for v in cycle):
                        bad += 1
            for v in path:
                visited[v] = 2
    print(ans + bad)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()

Need ensure path.index(cur) won't fail if cur is in path. It should, because visited[cur]==1 and we set visited=1 when appended. If cur is a node that was visited=1 from a previous traversal in the same DFS? There is no previous traversal in same DFS except current path. Since we only set visited=1 in current path and never reset, if visited[cur]==1, cur is in path. Good.

But wait, what if cur is a non-present node that was visited=1 from an earlier DFS? We set all path nodes to visited=2 after each DFS, so no visited=1 remains. So okay.

Now, let's test with a component where start from tail and reaches cycle node that was already visited=2 from earlier? Then no count. But if the cycle was already counted, fine. If the cycle was not counted because we started from a tail in a previous DFS and counted it, then all nodes in component visited=2. Good.

Now, let's think about if we start from a node in a component with a cycle and a tail, but the cycle was already counted when starting from a tail in the same DFS? We count once. Good.

Now, let's test with a->b,b->c,c->a,d->a. Start i=a: path [a,b,c,a], cur=a visited=1, cycle [a,b,c], len3, indeg a=2 (c,d), so not bad. Mark all visited=2. Start i=d: present d, visited=0, path [d], cur=a visited=2, no cycle. Mark d. bad0. ans changed4. Good.
Start i=d first: path [d,a,b,c,a], cur=a visited=1, cycle [a,b,c], not bad. Mark all. Good.

Now, test with pure cycle a->b,b->a and tail c->d,d->d. Start i=a: path [a,b,a], cycle [a,b], indeg a=1,b=1, bad1. Mark a,b. Start i=c: path [c,d,d], cur=d visited=1, cycle [d], len1, not bad. Mark c,d. bad1. ans changed a,b,c =3 +1=4. Good.

Now, test with self-loop a->a and tail b->a. Start i=a: path [a,a], cycle [a], len1. Mark a. Start i=b: path [b], cur=a visited=2, no cycle. bad0. ans changed b=1. Good.

Now, test with acyclic a->b,b->c,c not present. Start i=a: path [a,b,c], cur=-1, no cycle. Mark a,b,c. bad0. ans changed3. Good.

Now, test with a->b,b->a only, P=2, A=26. cntS=2, cntT=2, not -1. changed2. indeg a=1,b=1. DFS cycle len2 bad1. ans3. Good.

Final code:

```python
import sys

def solve():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data:
        return
    N = int(data[0])
    S = data[1]
    T = data[2]

    mp = [-1] * 26
    present = [False] * 26
    for ch in S:
        present[ord(ch) - 97] = True

    for a, b in zip(S, T):
        c = ord(a) - 97
        d = ord(b) - 97
        if mp[c] == -1:
            mp[c] = d
        elif mp[c] != d:
            print(-1)
            return

    cntS = sum(present)
    cntT = len(set(T))

    if cntS == 26 and cntT == 26:
        if any(mp[c] != c for c in range(26)):
            print(-1)
            return

    ans = 0
    for c in range(26):
        if present[c] and mp[c] != c:
            ans += 1

    indeg = [0] * 26
    for c in range(26):
        if present[c]:
            indeg[mp[c]] += 1

    visited = [0] * 26
    bad = 0

    for i in range(26):
        if present[i] and visited[i] == 0:
            cur = i
            path = []
            while cur != -1 and visited[cur] == 0:
                visited[cur] = 1
                path.append(cur)
                cur = mp[cur] if mp[cur] != -1 else -1

            if cur != -1 and visited[cur] == 1:
                idx = path.index(cur)
                cycle = path[idx:]
                if len(cycle) >= 2 and all(indeg[v] == 1 for v in cycle):
                    bad += 1

            for v in path:
                visited[v] = 2

    print(ans + bad)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```

Now, code detail: In DFS, when we traverse from a present node and reach a non-present node, we append it and then cur=-1. This marks non-present visited. If later another present node maps to this non-present node, the while loop stops because visited[cur]!=0. This is fine. But what if a non-present node is visited=1 from current path and then cur becomes -1, no cycle. We mark it visited2. Good.

Potential issue: If a present node maps to a non-present node that was already visited2, the while loop stops immediately. If the present node itself is part of a cycle? It can't be because its outgoing goes to non-present, so no cycle. Good.

Now, let's think about if the DFS could fail to count a cycle if we start from a node that is not in the cycle but in a component whose cycle was already visited2 from a previous traversal that started from a tail in the same component? We count the cycle when first encountered. If first traversal started from a tail, it reaches the cycle and counts it. If first traversal started from a cycle node, counts. If first traversal started from a node in a different component that points into this component? impossible, components disjoint. Good.

Now, let's think about if a component has a cycle and a tail, and we start from a tail. path = [tail, ..., cycle_node1, cycle_node2, ..., cycle_node1]. path.index(cur) gives first occurrence of cycle_node1, cycle includes cycle nodes only. Good. If the tail path enters the cycle at a node that was already visited? It's the first time in this DFS, so visited=1. Good.

Now, let's think about if a component has a self-loop and a tail, start from tail: path [tail, self, self], cur=self visited=1, idx=1, cycle=[self], len1, not bad. Good.

Now, let's think about if a component has a self-loop and a tail, start from self: path [self, self], cycle [self]. Good.

Now, let's think about if a component has a cycle and a tail, and the tail path enters the cycle, but the cycle was already visited2 from a previous traversal that started from a node in the same component? If previous traversal started from a node in the same component, it would have visited all nodes in the component? Actually our DFS marks all nodes in path visited2. If it started from a cycle node, it visits the cycle and marks cycle nodes visited2, but does it visit the tail? No, because tails point into cycle, not out. Starting from a cycle node, path stays on cycle, doesn't go to tails. So tails remain unvisited. Later starting from a tail, path reaches a cycle node visited2, stops. No cycle counted. That's fine because the cycle was already counted. But wait, if starting from a cycle node first, we count the cycle. Then starting from tail, no count. Good.

If starting from a tail first, path includes tail and cycle, counts cycle, marks all visited2. Then starting from cycle node, visited2 skip. Good.

Now, what about a component with a cycle and a tail where the tail has a branch? Functional graph no branch in outgoing, but a node can have multiple incoming. Starting from one tail path reaches cycle and counts. Other tails later reach visited2 cycle and stop. Good.

I will now output the final code.</think>```python
import sys

def solve():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data:
        return

    N = int(data[0])
    S = data[1]
    T = data[2]

    mp = [-1] * 26
    present = [False] * 26

    for ch in S:
        present[ord(ch) - 97] = True

    for a, b in zip(S, T):
        c = ord(a) - 97
        d = ord(b) - 97
        if mp[c] == -1:
            mp[c] = d
        elif mp[c] != d:
            print(-1)
            return

    cntS = sum(present)
    cntT = len(set(T))

    # If all 26 letters appear in both S and T, the mapping is a permutation.
    # Without an unused letter, a non-identity permutation is impossible.
    if cntS == 26 and cntT == 26:
        if any(mp[c] != c for c in range(26)):
            print(-1)
            return

    ans = 0
    for c in range(26):
        if present[c] and mp[c] != c:
            ans += 1

    indeg = [0] * 26
    for c in range(26):
        if present[c]:
            indeg[mp[c]] += 1

    visited = [0] * 26
    bad_cycles = 0

    for i in range(26):
        if present[i] and visited[i] == 0:
            cur = i
            path = []

            while cur != -1 and visited[cur] == 0:
                visited[cur] = 1
                path.append(cur)
                cur = mp[cur] if mp[cur] != -1 else -1

            if cur != -1 and visited[cur] == 1:
                idx = path.index(cur)
                cycle = path[idx:]

                # A "bad" cycle is a pure directed cycle of length >= 2.
                if len(cycle) >= 2 and all(indeg[v] == 1 for v in cycle):
                    bad_cycles += 1

            for v in path:
                visited[v] = 2

    print(ans + bad_cycles)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```
